The developer's third mechanism on r3893499716, deferred out of #227 as a second pull request's worth of work: a predicate no search algorithm covers is still answerable, by detecting what the other conditions do reach, spawning what was found into a copy of the world, evaluating the predicate there against real Body and SemanticAnnotations, and deleting what it rejects. It is what closes the gap #227 leaves open. A look reports sightings rather than the things a relation is written over - a MontessoriShapeDetection is a dataclass, not a Body - so #227 has a backend promise to check its own narrowing relations, and refuses any other relation outright. Spawning the sightings into an imagination world is what gives every other predicate something real to be evaluated against, so nothing has to be refused for want of a subject. Duplicates nothing: the roadmap's "Two items that meet at one type" records this mechanism as this item's alone. Widened 2026-09-02 at the developer's decision, taking r3915631447 from #238: MontessoriShapeDetection is renamed DetectedMontessoriShape and made a Role for MontessoriShape, and both halves belong here rather than on #238. Role[T] is pure composition and takes its role taker explicitly, so every detection needs a MontessoriShape - a HasRootBody annotation over a body in a world - and a look reports what it saw before anything of the sort is in the world. Spawning what was found into a copy of the world is exactly what this item builds, so this is where the role taker first exists. It also makes the gap above close by construction: a detection that is a role of a world entity gives every predicate a real subject, so nothing has to be refused for want of one. The rename waits here rather than landing on #238 for a second reason the developer weighed: it is 51 references across 10 files, and #232, #236 and #239 would each inherit it as a conflict. Let a predicate answer whether it holds, with the measurement behind it

In progress Open · Ready
predicates-answer-whether-they-hold sdt_predicates_answer_whether_they_hold #229 session ↗
  • Session default
  • Opus 5
  • Sonnet 5
  • Haiku 4.5
  • Fable 5
Notes
The developer's direction on 2026-08-31, correcting a call #227 made: a relation that computes a ratio should still be a Predicate. Its `__call__` answers a truth value, the ratio is read from a method of its own, and a threshold field with a default is what turns the one into the other. #227 had made InsideOf a SymbolicFunction instead, on the grounds that Predicate.__call__ must return bool and three call sites read the ratio - which is true of the mechanism and wrong about the intent. A threshold field answers both: the callers that want the number call the method, and a statement that wants the relation gets a predicate it can assert. Applies to every relation in reasoning/predicates.py of that shape, not only InsideOf: is_body_in_region answers the same kind of fraction, and the truth-valued relations that are only @symbolic_functions today - support, contact, visibility, reachability, occupancy - cannot be stated in a query at all while they are functions. krrood's own symbolic_callable_to_function is the sanctioned way to keep the function spelling working off one implementation, so no call site in coraplex, segmind or giskardpy has to change with it. Its own pull request off main, at the developer's direction, because it is a change to semantic_digital_twin's vocabulary rather than to perception: the same placement surface-finish-annotation already has. The knowledge-directed items that need it merge it in rather than waiting for it to land, which is this plan's standing rule for depends_on. perception-predicates-guide-the-search (#227) needs it and carries the InsideOf change this item overturns, so that branch takes this one in and drops its own. Resolved 2026-08-31. The duplication against #33 (eql-verbalization's p4-sdt-migration) is verified and settled at the developer's direction: this branch carries the predicate classes and #33 rebases onto it, porting its 34 reviewed wordings across rather than its own copy of the migration. #33 is 166 commits behind main and dirty in predicates.py specifically, so it owes that rebase either way and meets this version of the file whichever order the two land; its two open review decisions block it and do not block this, so folding the other way would have put the 2026-09-15 deadline behind them. Nothing of #33's is lost - queries.py, robot_predicates.py, phrase(), ignored_base_classes and the verbalization snapshot all survive its rebase. Two faults the resolve found and fixed. The CI failure was this branch's, not main's: renaming SupportedBy's fields broke semantic_annotations/mixins.py:942, which calls is_supported_by by keyword, and the local verification could not see it because that test module imports rclpy and cannot be collected outside a ROS container (1120178b). And the four Triple-based relations rendered ungrammatically, because Triple reads the verb off a class name that is not verb-first; each now states its own clause, taking the wordings #33's review already settled (0b1234a0). sdt has no verbalization snapshot on main to have caught them - that test arrives with #33.
▸ Review thread r3896606294 on `predicates.py:348` is answered and deliberately left open: `Reachable` now reads *"a HomogeneousTransformationMatrix is reachable by a Body"*, as the thread asked, but eql-verbalization decision 11 words the same relation *"a Pose is reachable for the kinematic chain rooted at <root> and ending at <tip>"*. Both make the pose the subject; they differ in whether root and tip are both named, and only the developer can say which of the two stands once #33 rebases onto this branch.

Work out how to answer a look from the request, instead of being handed a pipeline

how-to-look-concluded-from-the-request claude/plan-item-kickoff-perception-idzwsk #266 session ↗ ↗ Review

Notes
Raised by the developer on #238 (r3915356623) and added at his direction on 2026-09-02: "why does evaluating a query needs a pipeline? why do we need anything specified other than the request and the camera and the feed (images input)? this feels rigid and hard designed and coded ... Also check if we can make use of EQL-RDRs in there to generalize this (the rdr in pull requests stack which has pr 77 as the tip)." He is right, and what the pipeline is explains why. MontessoriPerceptionPipeline has six fields and they are not one kind of thing: table and lid, world and reference_frame are knowledge that of_world already reads from the world - a recording simply has no world, which is why recorded_setup writes them down; headroom is detector-parameters-from-knowledge's to conclude; and only the two detectors are genuinely hand-wired. So the pipeline is the residue of everything not yet knowledge-directed rather than a second configuration surface beside the world, and a backend taking the request, the camera and the frames alone is what removing that residue looks like. What this item builds is the last piece: a rule tree that concludes *how to answer a look* from the request itself - which detectors run over which surfaces - grown by an expert when a new kind of request turns up, rather than a pipeline configured in advance. That is the half of "extensibility with new situations through interaction with an expert" that #231 answered only for the choice of detector and left open for the choice of everything else. It stacks on #159 (EQLSingleClassRDR), whose fit, render_tree, corner-case store and model file are what the item is written around, and reaches it through detector-parameters-from-knowledge (#239), which already merges #159 in. Doing it on #238 was refused for the reason #231 refused it: merging #159 there adds 9,236 lines over 50 files to a pull request whose own change is a few hundred. Live state on 2026-09-02: #159 open, out of draft, no blocking label; #77 open, out of draft, mergeable_state clean, carrying integration-conflict. depends_on names the two items that supply what the pipeline carries and are hand-wired today. surfaces-found-by-looking is deliberately not a dependency: surfaces are already read from the world by of_world, so a live look needs nothing from it - what that item removes is the last model-read surface for a *recording*, which this item can be demonstrated without. Review round of 2026-09-05, four threads, resolved the same day as one redesign. The hand-written case class is gone and so is the attribute name spelled as a string: the rules are built from the statement *a request whose detector is to be worked out* (from_underspecified(a(SceneRequest)(detector=...))), so what is described and what is left open are read off it, and every condition is stated over the request itself - the two a rule reads are properties of SceneRequest computed from the detection_type it already carries. EQLSingleClassRDR.from_underspecified is on #159 and so on this branch's own base, against a-look-is-described-by-a-match's premise that it needs #77; what it lacked was somewhere to say where the rules are kept, so it takes a model_saver now, since a rule concluding a detector cannot be written to a model file at all. The detector concept is general and in krrood beside PerceptionBackend, which is where the developer placed #222's general half for the same reason: PerceptionDetector carries capability, the variable a detector states it over and answers, and the kind of look a detector answers is the type parameter it binds, read back through SubClassSafeGeneric. PieceDetector (#231) had its own copy of all of it - the duplication the review spotted - and now inherits it, losing 40 lines; WayOfLooking is SceneDetector and take is detect, so one operation has one name across both families. krrood gained a mimic family of its own and six tests, so the concept is exercised without the demo, and the mimic is where conditioning on what the sensor provides is demonstrated. A detector states identity comparison itself, since a value-comparing dataclass has no hash and a rule holds the detector rather than a name for one. Three things are put back to the developer rather than built, each with a reply on its thread: wiring a detector per part of one description (one detector answers a description today, and FindThePieces finds the board itself because the board says how far each surface reaches); conditions in the twin's own vocabulary for this family, which waits on #255 making a detection a Role over a world entity; and this family conditioning on the sensor, whose look is the request and whose rules are stated before any frame exists, so the case would have to carry what the source offers rather than one frame.

The digital twin models a surface's colour but not its finish, which is the property that decides whether colour segmentation works at all: the brushed steel table throws a diffuse, piece-coloured reflection with no sharp boundary anywhere, which is why the edge fit had to be written. Touches only semantic_digital_twin, so it can start off main immediately. Notes
Raised by the developer on 2026-08-30, watching the workspace clip show the floor, the chairs and whoever was standing at the table. Every surface in this plan is taken from a model - either a constant (recorded_setup.WORKSPACE, still the old 1.0 x 1.2 m guess) or the body's own collision shape (surfaces-from-world). Neither is a measurement of where the table really is, and the demo has already drifted away from its own model once: the board and the pieces do not stand where montessori_demo's hardcoded layout puts them, though the table's height agreed exactly. This item finds the surface in the picture instead, from the properties the twin already states about it - a large horizontal plane, mirror-finished (surface-finish-annotation), colourless, of about the modelled size - and the description is an entity query language expression, so which detectors run is compiled from the conditions rather than written by hand. That is choose-detection-method's claim turned on the surface itself, and it is what makes the workspace something the robot works out rather than something a person tuned. Evidence worth keeping before it starts: fitting the *table's* plane works on this sensor even though fitting the pieces does not. The point-cloud trial of 4b74460f8 measured a RANSAC plane holding 34% of 693k points on the bare steel and 69% with a mat, table points scattering +/-17 mm about it, while no piece stood out of that cloud at all. So a plane fit is a candidate for the surface and known not to be one for what rests on it, and the same trial's clustering must not be re-run for pieces. Also worth taking from the captures: everything detected across all six lies within x 0.57..0.91 and y -0.02..0.37, against a region of x 0.35..1.35, y -0.45..0.75. Deliberately after the deadline's critical path - the demo can run on a tuned workspace, and this replaces the tuning rather than unblocking anything. EQL traverses nested attributes and properties, measured before the change, so the case class was never needed for the engine's sake. Stating the description as a Match over semdt entities read by an EQL-RDR needs the #77 stack this branch is not on, and is proposed as the item a-look-is-described-by-a-match, since TargetOnSurface (#231) and RequestedLook (#266) are the same flattening and one mechanism removes all three. Calibrating the camera against the twin, and combining the two finders to do it, is proposed as an item of its own and arrives as a third SurfaceFinder rather than a change to either, with two measured caveats: fitting this table's height answers 0.8804-0.8811 m against the modelled 0.88, so there is about a millimetre of transform error to recover here, and #236's 0.865 board is a wrong model rather than a wrong pose, which a solve with only a transform to move would absorb. Correct where the camera stands by fitting what is seen to what the twin models

Not started No pull request yet
camera-pose-fitted-to-the-model None no pull request yet
  • Session default
  • Opus 5
  • Sonnet 5
  • Haiku 4.5
  • Fable 5
Notes
Added 2026-09-05 at the developer's direction on #259, folding the two threads he asked to keep together (r3940349876 and r3940351882): "since we have a 3d model of the world, we can use this information to maybe even calibrate the camera ... and find the best matching transformation that would make fit our expectation from the 3d modeled digital twin", and "so basically we can combine ModelledSurface with MeasuredSurface as well to get the best estimate and update our belief about the camera transformation accordingly and get the best fitting surface?" It arrives as a third SurfaceFinder rather than as a change to either of the two #259 ships. SurfaceFinder is the interface and SurfaceRules already chooses among whatever declares it can answer, so a finder that takes the world's statement and the depth together, solves for the transform that best explains both, and answers the surface plus a corrected camera pose slots in with one add_rule and no edit to what is there. Three measurements bear on it, and two of them cut against the obvious version. The camera transform is already good in z on this setup. #259's first build fitted the table's height as well as its extent and answered 0.8804 to 0.8811 m against the modelled 0.88 on all six captures - about a millimetre - and moving the plane by it moved every detection on every capture, up to 3.4 mm and 0.19 agreement, which is why that branch ships measuring the extent alone. So there is not much transform error left to recover from the table. Where the model and the world really disagree is not the transform. #236 measured the board mesh at 0.865 of the real board, a similarity fit with a 2.1-2.6 mm residual, and no rectification plane closes it, since doing so puts the holes below the table the board stands on. That is a wrong model rather than a wrong pose, and a solve with only a transform to move would absorb it into one. #236's own framing is the right one here too: a law that must hold, and then a hypothesis about which of the things it is written over is the one that is wrong. What genuinely is there to fit is over-constrained. The table plane holds about 34% of 693k points on the bare steel, 69% with a mat, and the board's own outline is measured every frame with its layout known exactly - two independent observations of one rigid transform, in a scene where a wrong one has visible consequences. So the fit is well posed; what it needs is to be told which side is trusted, per capture, since the recorded layout has already drifted in x and y while the height agreed exactly, and an unconstrained solve will happily explain a wrong board pose as a camera error. Two things it needs that #259 does not have. A camera pose is a value on one RgbdFrame, so a correction has nowhere to live between frames - the same gap #232 filled for pieces with BelievedPlace and a BeliefSource - and until it does, "update our belief accordingly" is a returned number nobody keeps rather than an operation. And the two finders are today layered rather than independent: the measurement never grows past what the model allows, only cuts it down, which is deliberate and is what keeps a run searching only ground the world described. Combining properly means letting the measurement move the model, which is a different contract and is why this is a new finder under a new rule rather than a tweak to MeasuredSurfaceFinder.

Choose the detector by rule from the target and the surface

In progress Open · Ready
choose-detection-method claude/choose-detection-method-gf64yp #231 session ↗
  • Session default
  • Opus 5
  • Sonnet 5
  • Haiku 4.5

Notes
The detector interface the backend dispatches to, chosen by a rule tree - krrood's ripple-down rules, whose conditions are already entity query language expressions - over properties of the surface and the target: a mirror-finish surface with a known-geometry prism selects the edge fit; a matte contrasting surface selects the cheaper colour blob, which works there and is faster; a target with no geometric model selects a learned detector. This is where perception measurably gets better as the robot knows more, and it is the item the paper's claim rests on. The developer noted on 2026-08-29 that krrood's ripple-down rules are not usable yet and expects them to become so through the RDR/EQL refactor's integration build; this item cannot start before they are, which is the schedule risk to watch on the 4-8 September window. Two corrections from 2026-08-31. The conditions the tree reads are not only standing properties of the surface and the target - what finish it has, whether the target has a geometric model - but also what has lately happened to the target: whether it is believed to be somewhere in particular, how sure that belief is, and what acted on it last. A rule that fires on "the target was released over this hole one action ago" is the same kind of rule as one that fires on "this surface is mirror-finished", and it is the kind the captures actually need. And the planned rule "a matte contrasting surface selects the cheaper colour blob" needs its other justification writing down, since detect-per-supporting-surface already recorded that the edge fit works on the lid too; speed is the honest reason, not capability. A third reading of the same claim arrived on #222 (r3893463818), and it is this item's rather than the backend's: "we can also have perception algorithms as classes that declare explicitly their capabilities, what requests they can answer to, also could be described as eql conditions, then a perception reasoner will choose the algorithm that has a capability that can answer that request." That is this item's rule tree with the condition moved onto the detector - each detector states the requests it can answer, and the choice is made by matching a request against those statements rather than by a tree written over the surface and the target. Worth settling which of the two shapes this item builds before it starts, since they answer the same question from opposite ends. Built 2026-08-31 as #231, 69f30348a. Both shapes were built, layered, at the developer's direction: each detector declares the looks it can answer as an entity query language condition, and the ripple-down tree chooses among the ones that can. The planned third rule - a target wearing the surface's own hue falls back to the edge fit - turned out not to be a rule but the colour blob's capability going false, so the tree ships the two rules the budget asks for. Speed is the honest reason and it is measured: the colour blob costs 89 ms against the edge fit's 126 ms on the same work, reporting the same pieces at comparable agreement. The recorded blocker was three claims and only two are true. The classic krrood.ripple_down_rules machinery is unusable as recorded (source-string conditions, an Expert required for every tree mutation), and EQLSingleClassRDR is eight unmerged pull requests deep in the D-core-* stack - but the EQL-native rule trees the item's own note describes are usable on main today, 24 tests passing with no skips. So this item was never blocked, and neither is detector-parameters-from-knowledge behind it. One structural finding: choosing a detector per piece means a surface is searched once per detector, and the first working version was slower annotated than not (0.596 s/frame against 0.521). Rectifying each plane and reading its edges once per frame rather than once per detector fixes it (0.494 against 0.491) and made the unannotated path faster too. Anything derived from the picture belongs to the frame once more than one detector answers a look. Nothing in this workspace states a finish yet, so every look still falls to the edge fit on the scenes as they stand - the same shape as surfaces-from-world's supporting_surface being None everywhere, and asserted directly. The rule sending an amber piece to the edge fit reads the colour the world states, and BOARD_COLOR is eleven hues from the wood the camera measures, so it does not fire on the real board until detector-parameters-from-knowledge moves measured colours onto the twin. Corrected 2026-08-31 by the developer: the EQL-native RDR engine *is* usable. The note above read the D-core-* stack's merge state as its usability, which contradicts this plan's own rule that depends_on means stacked on, never waiting for a merge. #64 -> #65 -> #66 -> #67 -> #98 -> #159 (EQLSingleClassRDR) -> #210 -> #79 -> #76 -> #80 -> #77 (the @rdr decorator) are all open, out of draft and reviewed. Only the classic krrood.ripple_down_rules machinery is unusable. #231 keeps its stated rule tree anyway, at the developer's decision: EQLSingleClassRDR.query is init=False so the engine grows its tree by fitting cases through an Expert rather than taking one already built, which would make two known rules fitted-from-examples; and merging #159 in would add 9,236 lines of another plan's stack to a 600-line pull request. What the engine adds - fit, render_tree, the corner-case store, the model file - is what the next two items are written around. Review round of 2026-08-31, answered as 92afdcd82. Two threads, and the second changed the design: building the rule tree inside the method that evaluates it leaves nothing for anything else to read, so it may as well have been an if, and the point of an EQL rule tree is that new situations can be added to it. The tree is stated once now, in DetectorRules.__post_init__, and a look is decided by binding it to the one variable every rule states its conditions over and evaluating the tree already held - the same rebind krrood's own GuardCondition.holds_for performs. add_rule attaches a new rule to the tree while it is in use, with Alternative.insert_at, beside the exceptions already stated so a look reaches at most one of them; a test pins that a rule added at runtime changes the next look's answer, and it fails if the tree is rebuilt per look. Each detector states its own capability once the same way, and capability() is an ordinary method typed ConditionType over a TargetOnSurface rather than Any over Any, which was the other thread. Choosing for one surface costs 1.0 ms rather than 4.4 ms as a result. 233 tests pass across the montessori modules against 230 before the round. What the round did not do is the other half of what it asked: nothing yet asks an expert when no rule fires, since the expert interface is on #98/#159/#76 rather than on main. The decision to stay off the engine is unchanged and is the developer's, and it is put back to him on the thread rather than reversed here - the thread is left open for that reason.

Describe a look by an underspecified match over the twin's own entities, not by a case class

Not started No pull request yet
a-look-is-described-by-a-match None no pull request yet
Notes
Added 2026-09-05 at the developer's direction on #259 (r3940339984 and r3940357436): "why not replace this with an EQL match statement of semdt semantic annotation or kinematic structure entities?", and "eql based rdrs (pr 77 for example) can take an underspecified match statement and get needed information from it to evaluate the rule tree of the rdr." Every rule tree on this plan states its conditions over a hand-written case class that flattens what the world already states, because the tree needs one variable to bind: TargetOnSurface on #231, RequestedLook on #266, and SoughtSurface on #259. The last of the three is what raised it, and reading it is what showed how bad the shape is - two of its three fields were a copy of WorkspaceSurface.finish and a constant that of set True unconditionally, so the base rule's condition could never be false. AGENTS.md's own rule against a value spelled in two places condemns each of them independently of any redesign. What replaces them is one mechanism rather than three separate corrections: the description a rule tree is stated over is an underspecified Match over the twin's own entities - a Body, a Region, a HasSupportingSurface - read by an EQL-based ripple-down rule tree that takes what it needs from the match. A rule then states a condition in the world's own vocabulary and nothing has to be flattened for it first. Stacks on #77, the tip of the RDR stack (#159 -> #210 -> #79 -> #76 -> #80 -> #77), which is why it is not folded into any of the three branches carrying a case class: #231 refused merging #159 twice over for the same reason, that it adds 9,236 lines over 50 files to a pull request whose own change is a few hundred, and #77 adds 22,745. depends_on names the three items whose case classes it removes, since none of them can be removed before it exists. One measurement to carry into it, taken on #259 while answering the same thread: EQL conditions traverse nested attributes and property reads - sought.surface.region.area > 0.0 and sought.frame.carries_depth both bind and evaluate. So a case class was never needed for the engine's sake even without the RDR stack, which is why the duplicated half of SoughtSurface could be removed on #259 and only the Match itself waits for this item. Corrected 2026-09-05 by #266's own review round: EQLSingleClassRDR.from_underspecified is on #159, not on #77, so the Match-reading half of this item does not need the tip of the RDR stack and #266 used it - the same shape of correction as the 2026-08-31 "unmerged is not unusable" one. What remains for this item is the other half, which is what actually needs the stack: a Match over the twin's own entities rather than over a plain description. RequestedLook no longer exists to be removed; TargetOnSurface and SoughtSurface still do. Measured while answering the same round: a case class was never needed for the engine's sake, since EQL traverses nested attributes and property reads, so what is left here is the vocabulary the conditions are stated in rather than the shape the engine demands.

Perception knows what should be true

What the robot did, and what Segmind saw it do, say where a thing should now be. Perception looks there, and reports a violated expectation as a failure rather than as an absence.

Notes
An expectation about where a thing now is comes from what was done to it, and on a recording nothing has been done to anything until the recording is replayed. Segmind already has the extension point: EpisodePlayer -> DataPlayer -> FilePlayer, with CSVEpisodePlayer and JSONPlayer as its two members, and a player supplies exactly one thing - a generator of frames, each frame a set of body poses. A player reading a rosbag's transform tree and joint states is that same member over a different file, and EpisodeSegmenterExecutor then produces the support, pick-up, placing and insertion events over it without changing. Touches only segmind, so it can start immediately and off main. A constraint measured on 2026-08-31 that decides what this can be asked for. Only one of the six recorded bags carries the robot at all: tracy_pickup_demo holds /tf, /tf_static, /joint_states and both arms' and both grippers' joint states over 150 seconds, 253,000 messages. The other five hold nothing but the three camera topics over about 29 seconds each. So a replayed episode gives an event history for the pick-up demo and for anything recorded from now on, and it gives the live demo its history directly, but it cannot supply one for the five scene captures. Those get their expectations from the board and the action model instead - the hole an insertion named is a place in the world, known without any recording - which is why pieces-looked-for-where-expected does not depend on this item. Worth recording for whoever runs it: recording /tf and /joint_states alongside the camera in future takes costs nothing and would remove this split entirely. Expect what the action promised, and report the difference as a failure

In progress Open · Draft
expectations-from-events claude/plan-item-kickoff-expectations-2zvpmn #257 session ↗ ↗ Review
  • Session default
  • Opus 5
  • Sonnet 5
  • Haiku 4.5
  • Fable 5
Notes
Segmind already emits SupportEvent, LossOfSupportEvent, PickUpEvent, PlacingEvent and InsertionEvent over is_supported_by. Perception keeps a set of expectations from them - after LossOfSupport(piece, table) stop searching the table; after Placing(piece, lid) search the lid - and the action model gets there first, since an action declares its own effect and so the expectation can be armed before the event confirms it. The same mechanism gives failure detection: perception looked exactly where the action promised, found nothing, and says so. Widened on 2026-08-31, at the developer's ask, from an expectation that names a surface to one that carries a pose. Naming the surface is the part the request language can push down, but it is not what the captures need: three of them - stuck_cube_in_hole, disoriented_cube_on_hole and displaced_cube_from_hole - are a cube that an insertion put at a *named hole*, and the hole is a place, known in the world, on a surface the board detection measures every frame. So an expectation is a belief about where an object is: a region it should lie in, an interval of yaw it should be turned through, and the surface it should rest on. That is precisely the hypothesis pieces-looked-for-where-expected evaluates, which is why the two are now linked. What the belief is propagated by is the action's own declared effect, confirmed or refuted by the events: released over a hole, an object is believed to be at that hole, turned any way, within the spread a release allows; still grasped, its pose is the gripper's; acted on by nothing, its pose is exactly where it was last seen. The rule that carries the weight is the last one - a belief only decays when something acts on the object - and it is why a history makes this tractable where a single frame does not. Failure detection then follows from the belief rather than from a surface: the insertion promised the cube would end up *in* the hole, and it is found resting *on* the lid, turned thirty degrees from where it would have had to be. Reporting which part of the expectation was violated is what a recovery can act on, and it is the paper's end-to-end story. Built 2026-09-03 as #257, over the merge of #232, #222 and #246 - three dependencies on three stacks, the first item on the plan whose dependencies span that many. An expectation is a named piece, a BelievedPlace, what it should be resting on and what put the belief there; where a look is aimed and what the piece should rest on are deliberately two fields, which is what makes a cube left lying on the lid a reportable failure rather than a sighting. SceneRequest carries what the asker believes, as this plan's note of 2026-08-31 predicted it would need to. 482 passed against 453 on the merge of the three, which is the 29 tests added and nothing else moved. The item's own reach exposed a fault worth more than the feature. This is the first belief on the plan that states how far it reaches - every one before it reached exactly SEED_REACH - and varying a radius showed the fit is not monotonic in it: PieceMatcher's grid was laid out from -radius, so its phase moved with the reach and a peak one reach landed on the next stepped over. That is #238's lattice finding in the sweep rather than the rectification; offsets_within counts outwards from the centre. The four lid expected-to-fail marks do not come off, and the measurement says why. Two of the four are a cube an insertion put at a named hole, so a history does say where to look and the cube is fitted - but not at every reach even with the grid monotone, because it reaches 0.645 against a ghost cylinder's 0.641 at the same place. Stating a reach that happens to work is the tuning this plan has refused three times, and separating a piece from a ghost that follows the same edges is competing-explanations' claim. LID_PIECES_STILL_MISSED now records that, and names that item. The review round of 2026-09-04 moved two things and pointed at a third. A pick-up means held, not supported: Segmind builds `PickUpEvent` with no `with_object`, so the old mapping answered nothing for a real pick-up and its test stated an event no detector emits. What an event says about support is now declared on the event classes in Segmind (`SupportEffect`, `EventWithSupportEffect`), and the expectation types hold the world's entities rather than names. The third is where the generalization the reviewer asks for already lives: the relation vocabulary of #229 and #238 on the other stack, which makes re-basing on #255 the honest answer to five of the threads rather than a second hierarchy here. Re-based 2026-09-04 at the developer's decision on #257, onto #255 with #246 merged in, so the branch now carries #222, #227, #229, #238 and #255 beneath it and depends_on names #255's item in place of perception-backend. The one conflict, reasoning/predicates.py against #244's numeric rewrite, is resolved by carrying #244's fast paths into #229's classes. An expectation is now the relations of the twin's own vocabulary stated about the piece with nothing standing in its place (SupportedBy the lid, InsideRegion the hole, Near the hole within the release spread), and nothing enumerates which: the same relations are what a look is asked for, a placement that confines the piece seeds a fit on the asker's say-so, and what the look finds is asked the same relations - each read off the sighting by one SightingReading per kind the look establishes itself, and off the body #255 stands in the imagined world otherwise - so the violated relations, written over that body, are the report. Each Segmind event declares its Effect as the relations that begin and end holding; the SupportEffect classes are gone, since RestingOn was a second name for SupportedBy. The twin gains Turned. The anchored sweep grid of the previous round had never been called by the sweep and is wired now. Verified against #255 in the same container: krrood 1142 passed against 1135 with the same 178 errors, experiments 516 passed against 478, segmind 64 passed, the twin's failing set unchanged but for two errors in #246's own armar7 tests that error on #246's tree too. The predicted-state item the review asked for is predicted-state-from-declared-effects. Review round of 2026-09-05, nineteen threads, resolved the same day. The expectation and the store are general concepts in Segmind now (segmind.expectations: Expectation, Expectations), with MontessoriExpectation and MontessoriExpectations in experiments carrying only how a look at the scene is asked for one and how a sighting is checked. An insertion's declared effect is InsideRegion the hole and Near it within the release spread, and no longer SupportedBy the lid: measured with the twin's own SupportedBy, a piece sunk in its hole is not supported by the plate the hole is cut through, so expecting it contradicted the insertion's own success; the look still reaches the lid alone, because the hole's region reaches from the lid's plane down into the board and the table pass has nothing of it to read. StatedRelation is an entity query language Match over the relation''s own class with the subject unstated - type, kwargs, construct_instance and the match''s attribute test are what its four operations were, so what is left is which operand is the subject, which the object, and reading one off a statement''s condition; it defines equality as mutual coverage, since a Match is eq=False and a stated relation is compared as a value. StatedRelation.stated_in reads every relation a statement asserts, and read_request uses it. ▸ One thread is open on #257 and it is the developer's to settle. r3940953108 asked whether an event's declared effect duplicates Segmind's statechart, and on 2026-09-05 he proposed making InsideOf answer for regions as the fix. Measured on the plate scene: it already does - Region is a KinematicStructureEntity and every attribute the reading touches is on that base - answering 1.000 sunk in the hole, 0.500 standing on the plate over it and 0.000 lifted clear, at 23x less cost than InsideRegion. But it counts vertices inside a bounding box rather than volume inside a shape, so its own default of 0.5 reads the over-the-hole case as in the hole, while the detectors' 0.9 reads it correctly. So the fold works at 0.9, and what is his to decide is whether the threshold may carry that correctness and whether InsideOf gains the allowed_space InsideRegion has as a PlacementRelation.

Predict the twin's current state from what the actions and the events declared, and ask perception from it

Not started No pull request yet
predicted-state-from-declared-effects None no pull request yet
Notes
Added 2026-09-04 at the developer's ask on #257 (r3934932647 and r3937639689). expectations-from-events keeps what holds of one piece the robot acted on, from the action's declared effect and each event's declared Effect, both stated as the twin's own relations. This is the general form of that: a predicted current state of the digital twin, built by applying every declared effect of the actions performed and the events seen to the world the robot has - explicit effect and occurrence rules, each event and each action saying queryably what now holds and what no longer does, kept inside what the domain model can express so the frame problem of situation calculus does not arise - from which perception is asked richer questions: the gripper no longer holding the piece, the piece resting where it was left, supported by the surface it was left on. Where an effect cannot be enumerated in advance it is concluded by the EQL-native ripple-down rules on the #77 stack, so knowledge is added as rules rather than as code. An action's pre- and post-conditions in coraplex are the same statements for actions, and where the two meet is worked out here. The prediction is what the simulation and the twin's own state should already agree with when the plan was executed there; the case it exists for is another agent acting, or the world disagreeing, which is what a look is then asked about. It meets icra-experiments' snapshot-working-memory and failure-taxonomy-and-typing, which are the state and the failure side of the same thing; which of the three owns which half is the developer's to settle when it starts. Probabilistic fusion (a Kalman or particle filter) is not part of it: the relations are true or false and a belief is confirmed or refuted, and the accurate pose is a different question the paper's claim does not need.
needs expectations-from-events

Notes
One adapter implementing the detector interface over a RoboKudo analysis engine, so the rule tree can choose a learned detector (RoboKudo ships CLIP and YOLO annotators) for a target with no geometric model. Proves the reasoning layer is executor-agnostic and is the route by which this work moves into RoboKudo after the deadline. Deliberately last and expected to fall after it: nothing else depends on it, no demo item needs it, and it is the first thing to drop if the deadline tightens.
